News

Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Oil slides as G7 says it will release 100 million barrels of reserves
G7 enforces 100 million barrel release timeline
Diesel cracks drop after announcement
States ease restrictions on dyed fuel use
Oil prices moved lower on Oct. 2 as near-term supply outlooks strengthened after the G7 announced it would enforce a fourmonth timeline for member states to release 100 million barrels of oil stocks they had previously committed to in March.
NYMEX November WTI settled $1.76/barrel lower at $91.11/b, and ICE December Brent declined 6 cents/b to $102.25/b.
In a statement released Oct. 2, the G7 called on the International Energy Agency to "monitor the immediate and full implementation of the March 2026 commitments."
The decision included a commitment for the G7 and partner countries to frontload a "substantial diesel release" in the next 20 days, with members set to discuss the possibility of additional releases in coordination with the IEA over the coming days, the statement said.
"Europe has just agreed to release a massive amount of their heavily stocked Diesel Oil," US President Donald Trump said in a post to his social media platform Truth Social prior to the G7 releasing its statement. "The process will begin immediately."
Oil prices, and especially diesel cracks, moved sharply lower immediately following the announcement, but steadily pared declines later in the session. The frontline NYMEX ULSD crack versus WTI fell to a session low $95.46/b in midmorning New York trading, but climbed to $97.94/b at market settle.
(continued on page 24)

International Crude
Prompt European sour crudes bearish, US SPR creates medium-term uncertainty
Johan Sverdrup falls over $10/b across four sessions as market lengthens
Sour differentials weakened in the wake of the previous SPR release
Release should see knock-on effects across global sour complex
A lengthening European sour crude balance, exacerbated by the potential release of sour US Strategic Petroleum Reserve barrels, continued to pressure differentials downward in the week to Oct. 2. However, supply balances ahead remain uncertain with traders expecting a mixed impact from SPR releases and Middle Eastern inflows.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the differential for Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad at a $14.375/b premium over Dated Brent Oct, 1, having fallen more than $10/b from the end of the previous week.
European sour crude differentials had begun wavering over the previous week, with increased arbitrage inflows from the Middle East and the Americas pointed toward the continent and a weakening sour market in Asia.
Increase in exports from the Persian Gulf have already put pressure on sour grades delivered into the Mediterranean, with Iraq's Basrah Medium heard close to flat against Dated Brent for November-arrival into the Mediterranean -- Octoberarrival values were heard around premiums of $5/b the previous week.
The downward pressure was intensified by the US Department of Energy announcing that it will offer up to 40 million barrels of sour crude to be released in November and December. Previous releases from the US SPR in April and May had resulted in weaker sour crude diffentials in Europe. .

Mixed impact from US SPR releases However, while the US SPR announcement had caused
an immediate bearish effect on prompt differentials, traders were mixed regarding the medium-term impact on the European market.
On the one hand, traders contrasted the relative availability of alternative sour crudes from the Middle East and Americas for

the upcoming cycle compared with the tight supply environment during previous SPR releases.
The last time the US SPR grades came to the Med, "Hormuz grades were not coming," a Europe-based crude trader said. "It does not seem that the market needs the SPR barrels now."
In addition, the US SPR barrels, such as Big Hill and Bryan Mound, have high residual yields compared with European sours of choice like Johan Sverdrup, Kirkuk or Basrah Medium. With the Mediterranean already past the summer bitumen season, refiners are unlikely to prioritize the US grades, which would also require more intensive processing.
According to the crude assays made available on the US Department of Energy website, Bryan Mound commingled sour crude has an API gravity of around 33.3 and a sulfur content of 1.44%, while Bright Hill has an API gravity of 31 and a sulfur content of 1.84%.
European refiners had also noted quality issues associated with refining US SPR barrels in previous cycles, with the blended nature of the crude giving rise to significant variability around expected parameters.
Issues around hydrogen sulfide had also been reported in the market at the time, and the publicly available assays reveal higher residue concentrations of nickel and vanadium, which could make catalytic cracking more challenging.
On the other hand, even if Europe does not see US SPR sour barrels offered into the region, the release of the barrels should still create knock-on impacts across the global sour complex, adding to the bearish momentum supported by a recovery in sour exports from the Gulf.
"[Even if] more US SPR stays local, we will see more Mars and Southern Green Canyon exported to Europe as a result," said a third trader.
Traders have noted rising exports of US Gulf sour grades since March, as the US-Iran war reshapes global trade flows. Southern Green Canyon, for instance, was heard offered into the Mediterranean at a $6/b premium to Dated Brent in late September, competing with regional sour grades.
-- Natasha Tan, Jan Kedzior
Urals flows to China reach a five-month high as Indian imports fall
China's imports of Urals surge to 7.4 mil barrels
Indian Urals imports fall 27% from July
Middle East flows to India rise, pressures Urals
China's imports of Russian Urals climbed to a five-month high in September, with half of the volume coming through the Northern Route, tracking data showed.
In September, 7.4 million barrels of Urals arrived in China, the highest since April, when imports stood at 10.9 million barrels, Kpler data showed Oct. 2. Half of the volume imported last month sailed through the Arctic Sea -- a route that is only open in the Northern Hemisphere's summer months. The journey from the

Baltic Sea to China normally takes up to two months via the Suez Canal, but the Northern Route shortens the journey to just over a month.
Meanwhile, Urals discharges have fallen for the second month in a row, with the main buyer, India, continuing to reduce its purchases.
Indian imports of Urals reached an all-time high of 63.2 million barrels in July but have since fallen to 46 million barrels -- a decrease of 27% -- even as total crude flows into India rose by 3.9 million barrels to 163.3 million barrels between July and September, Kpler data showed.
The redirection of some of the Urals flows from India to China coincided with an increase in flows from the Middle East to India, which reached 82.7 million barrels in September - near the 86.9 million barrels seen in February before the US-Israel war with Iran began, and much above the 51.5 million barrels reported in August.
Improved availability of Middle Eastern sour barrels for Indian refiners suppressed delivered sour prices into India, making Urals less attractive to prospective buyers.
"There is so much supply coming from the Middle East at the moment that Urals are no longer economically attractive, not to mention the risk of sanctions," one Asia-based source said.
The recent spike in spot prices in Europe could also have impacted the attractiveness of Russian barrels. Urals, when offered, typically price at a differential to the average price of Dated Brent for the month in which the cargo is loaded. With the Dated Brent pricing a few dollars above the Dubai benchmark, some Brent-linked cargoes could prove too expensive for some Asia-based buyers who have the option to buy cargoes which price from Platts Dubai, market sources said.
-- Jan Kedzior
Japan's JERA launches JERA Oil Storage for national petroleum reserves
Operating four national petroleum stockpiling terminals
Business transfer to be completed by April 2029
Petroleum reserves play key role amid Middle East conflict
Japan's largest power generation company, JERA Co. Inc., said Oct. 2 that it has established a petroleum reserve company, JERA Oil Storage Co. Inc., to consolidate national petroleum stockpiling operations.
The company said the move follows JERA's affiliated subsidiaries' operation of national oil reserves under a joint contracting arrangement with the Japan Organization for Metals and Energy Security.
In its announcement, JERA said it plans to conclude an absorption-type company split agreement with JERA Oil Storage by the end of 2026, under which JERA's oil stockpiling business is expected to be transferred to JERA Oil Storage around April 2029.
JERA added that the latest development aims to further enhance safe and stable operations and to ensure the transfer

and advancement of operational expertise by establishing an operating structure dedicated exclusively to the oil stockpiling business.
JERA's subsidiaries have been operating four national petroleum stockpiling terminals in Tomakomai-Tobu, Akita, Fukui and Shibushi. Through their accumulated expertise in equipment maintenance, safety management and emergency response, they have contributed to the stable operation of the national petroleum stockpiling bases, the company said.
Currently, JERA subsidiaries TOMATOH OIL STORAGE. CO. LTD., AKITA OIL STORAGE CO. LTD., FUKUI OIL STORAGE CO. LTD., SHIBUSHI OIL STORAGE CO. LTD. and Nexeraise Co. Ltd. operate the national petroleum stockpiling terminals under a JOGMEC contracting agreement through fiscal year 2028-29 (April-March), JERA spokespeople said.
The national petroleum stockpiling terminals have crude oil storage capacities of 6.4 million kiloliters, or 40.25 million barrels, at Tomakomai-Tobu; 4.5 million kiloliters at Akita; 3.4 million kiloliters at Fukui; and 5 million kiloliters at Shibushi, JERA spokespeople added.
JERA said its subsidiaries will continue to contribute to Japan's energy security and stability by operating national petroleum stockpiling bases while ensuring the safe and efficient management of the facilities and maintaining a system for the timely and efficient release of petroleum during emergencies.
The move by JERA comes at a time when Japan's use of petroleum reserves has helped the country weather the conflict in the Middle East.

In March, Japan decided to release the equivalent of about 50 days' worth of national petroleum reserves, while also implementing a 15-day reduction in required private-sector inventories and releasing about six days' worth of jointly held oil stocks maintained with oil-producing countries, as tanker traffic through the Strait of Hormuz was nearly halted. The Middle East accounted for 94% of Japan's crude oil imports in 2025, according to data from the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry.
The country's petroleum reserves were equivalent to 197 days of domestic consumption as of Sept. 29, comprising 102 days of national oil reserves, 91 days in privately held reserves and four days in a joint crude storage program with oil-producing countries, according to METI data released Oct. 2.
-- Takeo Kumagai
CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex cash differentials strengthen
One Dubai partial trade during MOC
Key sour crude markets strengthen
The Middle East sour crude complex saw no convergences during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 2, while cash differentials for key sour crude markets rose.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai and cash Murban both at a premium of $14.605/b to samemonth Dubai futures at the market close, both up $1.91/b on the day.

Americas Crude
USGC sour crude Mars bounces back from record low as export buying interest swirls
Mars premium to WTI surges $1.75/barrel
International buyers snap up cheap US sours
Refining margins favor light over heavy crude
US Gulf Coast sour crude oil differentials climbed Oct. 2 as sources said export buying helped boost values following recordlow prices seen a week ago.
Sour crude benchmark Mars was seen trading as high as cash WTI plus $2.50/barrel late Oct. 1, then backed off slightly Oct. 2, though it remained elevated throughout the day.
Platts assessed November Mars at a $1.50/b premium to cash WTI on Oct. 2, based on a trade last heard done at that level. That put Mars up $1.75/b on the day.
Mars has been recovering from an all-time low of cash WTI minus $10/b, assessed on Sept. 25. Competing imports of heavier, sour grades from places such as Canada and Venezuela, ongoing weakening of fuel oil prices, and lagging refining margins have put downward pressure on Mars.
Historically high diesel crack spreads have pushed the refining value of lighter crudes, which yield more diesel, well above heavy crudes like Mars. The Mars cracking yield was last at $39.73/b on Oct. 1, while WTI Midland in Houston was at $54.69/b, according to S&P Global Energy data.
Mars and other sours dipped earlier in the week after the US Department of Energy announced that it would offer up to 40 million barrels of sour crude from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve in order to curb global supply tightness due to the US/ Iran war. The SPR barrels are to be delivered in November and December. But the lower prices for US Gulf Coast sours have attracted international buyers and, in turn, pushed differentials up once again.
"It was getting cheap enough that the arb was open and people were shopping around," one Gulf Coast crude trader said.

A 1 million-barrel/day crude oil pipeline to the Canadian Pacific Coast, from the prime oil sands producing region of Alberta, has received a major boost, with the federal government listing the proposed infrastructure as a "project of national importance" and kickstarting an environmental and regulatory process, along with engineering works and stakeholder consultations, top government officials said Oct. 1.
A project of national importance would signify that the federal government-backed Major Projects Office now has sole authority to handle regulatory permits and grant approvals within a specified timeline.
"We didn't get here overnight, and the announcement today comes after an MoU we signed last November," Alberta Premier Danielle Smith told reporters on a webcast from Fort McMurray, the heart of the oil sands producing areas of Athabasca, Cold Lake and Peace River. "In July, we submitted a project application for the West Coast pipeline for being listed as a project of national interest, and our aim now is to start construction on Sept. 1, 2027."
The pipeline is now formally to be called Pacific Link (compared with West Coast earlier) and will sustain over 3.5 million b/d of crude oil production from the oil sands region, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said on the same webcast, adding the new pipeline will primarily target Asia and also provide an opportunity for Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin to wean away from its traditional market of the US and find new customers.
"This will be a tremendous opportunity for oil sands producers to narrow the price differentials and reach out to a new market," Carney said. "There's a structural challenge of energy security and the existential challenge of climate change. And these challenges are forcing countries to think differently of where their energy comes from and on who they can rely to supply."
Clear path forward A year ago, the planned pipeline was a vision, but today "we
have a clear path forward," Smith said, adding the construction of Pacific Link will result in the province's oil sands producers utilizing the latest technology to add incremental barrels through what would likely be brownfield expansions.

The West Coast pipeline will originate in Bruderheim, Alberta, and terminate at the Roberts Bank terminal in the southwest coast of British Columbia, the Alberta government said separately in a release, adding the proposed infrastructure will be "largely" following the existing 890,000-b/d Trans Mountain pipeline corridor to a deep-water, VLCC-capable port terminal at Roberts Bank.
Leveraging this existing corridor will significantly reduce regulatory and logistical barriers, while minimizing land disturbances, it said.
The West Coast pipeline will be a private-public partnership, with participants including the Alberta Petroleum Marketing Commission (representing the provincial government), Trans Mountain Corp. (representing the federal government) and Calgary-based midstream player Pembina Pipeline Corp., Smith said.
"The PONI listing provides greater certainty through a clearer, more efficient and predictable federal review process that will maintain environmental standards and Indigenous consultation, which will be led by the Major Projects Office and supported by the Canada Energy Regulator," Pembina Pipeline Corp. said in a statement on Oct. 1.
As domestic and global demand for secure and responsibly produced energy continues to grow, Canada has a tremendous opportunity to strengthen its role as a trusted supplier to allies and trading partners around the world, while helping Canadians capture greater value from our abundant natural resources, Lisa Baiton, president and CEO of the Canadian Association of Petroleum Producers, said in a statement.
West Coast crude faces headwinds September saw steeper discounts for US West Coast crude
grades, as geopolitical supply risk remained high. Pacific refiners compare Alaska North Slope and Canadian
barrel prices with other grades -- particularly Venezuelan barrels in the current climate -- so the market is susceptible to changes in regional demand, tanker freight rates, sanctions, and competing crude prices.
Platts assessed Pacific Cold Lake at an $11.71/b discount to the NYMEX crude CMA on Oct. 1, up 20 cents day on day. Pacific Dilbit was also assessed $1/b lower at minus $12.71/b, up 20 cents from the previous session. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Platts last heard ANS at a plus-$10.75/b premium to the NYMEX crude CMA on Sept. 21 and has assessed it unchanged at that level since then.
The Westridge/Cold Lake Edmonton spread has hovered around plus $10/b since mid-September, as well.
Recent wider Pacific Cold Lake and Pacific Dilbit discounts could also be impacted by weak regional refinery demand due to fall maintenance schedules. Unplanned outages or reduced runs can leave more prompt crude competing for fewer refinery slots.

-- Ashok Dutta, James Jones
ALASKA DATA: September oil output rises on Pikka gains
Santos, Repsol increase output at Pikka North Slope field
Pikka output reached 70,000 b/d at end of September
Companies near phase one goal of 80,000 b/d for Pikka
Alaska North Slope production gained sharply in late September as Santos Ltd. throttled up output at its new Pikka field on the western North Slope.
As of Sept. 27, Pikka was producing 70,000 b/d and nearing its first phase target of 80,000 b/d, a Santos official said, asking not to be identified. Pikka is owned 51% by Australia-based Santos and 49% by Repsol, based in Madrid.
Alaska Department of Revenue production data showed North Slope production gaining 49,817 b/d between Sept. 1 and Sept. 30, reaching 551,247 b/d on the final day of the month. The increase came mainly in the last few days of September as the Santos increased the injection of seawater into the Pikka reservoir, which stimulated production of crude oil.
Pikka's oil is metered by the state revenue department through the Kuparuk field pipeline system.
Santos previously said it expects to achieve the phase-one goal of 80,000 b/d by the third quarter of the year. The company said it is planning a phase two expansion at Pikka with the addition of more wells and facilities, which could bring Pikka to about 160,000 b/d when it is complete.
The phase two expansion has not been officially announced, but company officials have said that the engineering and planning are underway. Santos and Repsol have also discovered new oil deposits south of the Pikka field and are now doing the planning and engineering for the development of those.
Other North Slope fields performed as expected in September, according to the revenue department data. Prudhoe Bay, the largest field on the slope, averaged 285,914 b/d in September, up from a 263,548 average in August. September production is roughly on par with September, 2025, according to the data.

October 2, 2026

The increase was expected because field production facilities tend to perform better as cooler weather begins in autumn on the North Slope. Prudhoe is operated by Hilcorp Energy and owned in roughly one-third shares by Hilcorp, ConocoPhillips and ExxonMobil.
The small Lisburne field produced an average of 16,651 b/d in September, on par with a 16,508 b/d average in August but down somewhat year over year from an average of 17,388 b/d in September, 2025. Lisburne's production includes output from smaller satellite accumulations, which is metered along with the Lisburne field itself. Hilcorp Energy is the owner and operator at Lisburne, which is located in the eastern part of the Prudhoe Bay field, also operated by Hilcorp.
The Alpine field, the farthest west of the North Slope producing fields and located near the new Pikka field, continued to lag production in September, according to the revenue department data. Alpine produced an average of 38,877 b/d compared with an average of 39,300 b/d in August. Alpine is down an average of 3,900 b/d year-over-year from September 2025. Alpine is owned and operated by ConocoPhillips.
Alpine production has been affected by problems in managing rising gas-oil ratios in nearby satellite fields, GMT-2 and GMT-1, in the federally owned National Petroleum Reserve-Alaska. Mixed oil and gas fluids and water are shipped by pipeline to processing facilities in the nearby Alpine field.
As gas volumes increase in the mixed fluids, the field operators at Alpine must reduce crude oil production to manage the gas. ConocoPhillips also operates and owns the small GMT-1 and GMT-2 satellites.
In other developments, ConocoPhillips is continuing construction of its Willow field in the NPR-A and northwest of the producing fields, which are on state of Alaska lands. Willow is planned for completion and startup in early 2029 and is expected to produce 180,000 b/d at peak, ConocoPhillips has said.

Gasoline
Brazil October gasoline imports thin amid domestic oversupply
Russia leads Brazil's October gasoline imports
Itaqui port receives most gasoline volumes
Brazil is expected to receive at least 125,784 cubic meters of gasoline imports through Oct. 4, according to preliminary data from port lineups, sources, and S&P Global Commodities at Sea released Oct. 2.
At least three ships are scheduled and confirmed to discharge imported gasoline at Brazilian ports by October. Most will already have arrived, berthed, or discharged.
Russia is set to be the top country of origin, accounting for 64,151 cubic meters, or 51%, of October imports, and the Netherlands is expected to be the second-largest importer, with 61,151 cubic meters, or 49%.

Despite Russia's ban on fuel exports to secure domestic needs, two cargoes of Russia-origin gasoline appear to be arriving in Brazil. Those are exported from Russia as naphtha and are blended in transit to meet Brazilian A-grade gasoline specifications, which have low octane numbers due to the country's mandatory final 32% anhydrous ethanol blend.
For these international gasoline imports, the top two discharge ports are Itaqui (93,651 cubic meters) and Santos (32,133 cubic meters).
The Brazilian gasoline import arbitrage window has been closed for 219 days, according to an Oct. 2 report from the Brazilian Association of Fuels Importers, Abicom. Across Brazil's six primary transport fuel trading hubs, domestic prices average about 800 reais/cubic meter below import-parity prices.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Itaqui all-origin gasoline at $2.7801/gallon, with ARA as the most competitive origin, at a 62.25-cent discount to NYMEX November RBOB.
-- Beatriz Correr
European gasoline blendstocks, octane boosters rally in September
Butane prices surge on tight supply MTBE hits highest level since July 2022 Winter demand, lower imports drive blending rally
European gasoline blendstock and octane booster markets closed September sharply higher Sept. 30, as strong blending demand and limited prompt supply lifted Northwest European butane to monthly highs and MTBE to its highest level since July 2022.
Northwest European butane values rallied through September, with both FOB NWE and CIF NWE Seagoing reaching monthly highs by the end of the month amid strengthening winter gasoline blending demand and persistently tight prompt supply.
Platts assessed FOB NWE Seagoing butane at $954/mt on Sept. 30, up $371/mt from $583/mt at the start of September, while CIF NWE Seagoing butane rose $302/mt over the month to $908/mt from $606/mt.

Market participants increasingly focused on winter gasoline specifications as September progressed, supporting demand for butane as a blending component. The seasonal shift toward winter-grade gasoline improved blending economics and generated additional demand from refiners seeking barrels for internal consumption.
At the same time, spot market availability remained constrained. Refiners were heard retaining greater volumes for captive gasoline blending requirements rather than placing material into the merchant market, limiting prompt supply and underpinning values across Northwest Europe.
"There are no tonnes available in the market," one market participant said, describing butane as "very expensive." The resulting reduction in spot availability contributed to a steadily firmer market structure through much of the month.
Buying interest intensified during the second half of September as market participants sought to secure prompt barrels ahead of the winter gasoline blending season, while limited spot availability amplified upward price momentum.
FOB NWE butane outperformed CIF NWE during the rally, widening the FOB premium to around $46/mt at month-end from roughly $23/mt at the beginning of September.
By Sept. 30, FOB NWE butane had gained nearly 64% month on month, while CIF NWE butane had increased almost 50%, capping a month in which strengthening winter-specification gasoline demand and reduced spot supply combined to drive values sharply higher.
Prompt prices in the European MTBE market also climbed across the month as tightness in the gasoline market underpinned favorable economics for blenders.
Platts assessed MTBE FOB ARA at $1,719.25/mt Sept. 30, up $179/mt from the end of August, at its highest since July 4, 2022.
Despite the seasonal transition to winter spec gasoline, blending demand for MTBE remained healthy. One producer described blending economics as "great", pointing to persistently wide gasoline-naphtha spreads as supportive.
Fewer prompt import arrivals have also driven greater intraregional demand for T2 material.
"MTBE is tight due to less imports and decent blending demand," a trading source said.
High Asia-Europe freight rates and long lead times have

deterred importers from bringing T1 material, while Chinese producers have also looked to prioritize domestic needs.
Market tightness has been further driven by lower producer output. Operating rates in Europe have been heard between 7075% and strain on the barge supply chain deriving from low Rhine river levels has impacted shipments to Northwest Europe.
Overall volumes traded in the Platts Market on Close assessment process increased in September, rising to 6,000 mt from 1,000 mt the previous month.
"There's been decent buying for barges in Europe... demand exceeds supply," another trading source said.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Callum Townsend, Baran Serdaroglu
California gasoline stocks rise on blending components, refinery output flat
Blending components soar 523,000 barrels
Refinery production stalls at 4.87M barrels
Inventories remain 15% below last year's level
California's total gasoline inventories rebounded sharply during the week ended Sept. 25, fueled by a surge in blending components and out-of-state grades, even as refinery production stagnated at multi-week lows, according to California Energy Commission data from Oct. 1.
Total gasoline stockpiles jumped by 540,000 barrels over the week to reach 9.69 million barrels, marking their highest level since mid-August. Despite the sizable build, the state's fuel reserves remain well below historical benchmarks, trailing the 11.43 million barrels held a year ago by more than 15% and lagging the 10.44 million barrels recorded two years prior.
The weekly inventory accumulation was driven almost entirely by secondary fuel categories. Gasoline blending components led the charge, soaring by 523,000 barrels to hit 4.40 million barrels. Out-of-state supplies also mounted a strong recovery, with nonCalifornia gasoline stocks expanding by 170,000 barrels to settle at 781,000 barrels.
The gains comfortably absorbed a pullback in the state's primary domestic fuel. CARB reformulated gasoline stocks snapped a three-week streak of builds, sliding by 153,000 barrels to land at 4.51 million barrels.
Meanwhile, activity on the refinery floor was steady. Total refinery inputs and production remained completely flat weekon-week at 4.87 million barrels, holding well beneath the 5-million-barrel threshold. Refining throughput remains deeply depressed compared to historical volumes, falling far short of the 6.05 million barrels churned out a year ago and the 6.11 million barrels produced two years ago.
Individual manufacturing lines mirrored the broader operational freeze. In-state CARB reformulated gasoline output held unchanged at 4.32 million barrels for the week, while production of non-California export grades also plateaued at 548,000 barrels.

SINGAPORE DATA: Light distillate stocks fall 11% WOW to 10.51 mil barrels
Net gasoline exports up 38% WOW
Imports of naphtha, reformates, blendstocks down 33%
Singapore's commercial stockpiles of light distillates fell 10.71% week over week over Sept. 24-30 amid higher net gasoline exports and lower naphtha imports, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 1.
Stocks of light distillates -- which include gasoline, reformates and naphtha but exclude gases like LPG -- totaled 10.514 million barrels in the week ended Sept. 30, the data showed.
The city-state's gasoline exports rose 29.27% week over week to 710,855 metric tons in the week to Sept. 30, while gasoline imports were up 13.51% at 228,338 mt over the same period.
The rise in gasoline exports came despite lower outflows to Indonesia, which fell 30.89% week over week to 159,601 mt. However, exports to Australia rose more than fivefold to 213,239 mt over the same period.
Looking ahead, Indonesia's demand for prompt gasoline cargoes was seen as firm, with state-owned Pertamina Patra Niaga last seeking 9.1 million barrels of 90 RON gasoline for loading or delivery over November-December and 1.3 million barrels of 92 RON gasoline for loading or delivery over the same period via tenders that closed Sept. 24.
Meanwhile, the rise in gasoline imports came despite lower inflows from China, which fell 21.49% week over week to 78,162 mt.
Some market participants expect China's gasoline exports to drop sharply in October amid energy security concerns and stronger domestic demand during the Golden Week holidays.
Net gasoline exports rose 38.36% week over week to 482,517 mt in the week ended Sept. 30, according to the data.

Imports from India, Italy and Saudi Arabia totaled 33,585 mt, 29,566 mt and 27,682 mt, respectively, following no inflows from the three countries the previous week.
Meanwhile, Singapore's exports of naphtha, reformates and other blendstocks rose nearly eightfold week over week to 149,709 mt in the week to Sept. 30.
Most of the outflows were destined for China, at 74,999 mt, and Thailand, at 65,203 mt, for reexport purposes, while domestic exports to Malaysia stood at 9,507 mt.
-- Joshua Ong, Zoey Ng
Jet
INTERVIEW: Geopolitics reframes SAF as strategic asset, not just green fuel: Vopak
EU traceability rules seen restricting Rotterdam-Schiphol flows
Dutch HBE scheme drives localized voluntary SAF blending
ReFuelEU review should tackle logistics bottlenecks
Geopolitical instability has recast sustainable aviation fuel as a strategic energy security asset, beyond being a clean fuel transition, but fragmented EU pipeline regulations are hampering supply chains, an official at storage firm Royal Vopak said.
Higher conventional jet fuel prices driven by global instability have shifted the industry's perception of SAF, elevating it from a green-transition instrument to a tool for national and regional fuel autonomy, Nikki Schutte, senior vice president for business development in the Netherlands at Vopak, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in an interview Oct. 2.
"Geopolitical instability has driven conventional jet fuel prices higher and transformed the role of SAF," Schutte said. "It elevated energy security, shifting the view of SAF from purely a green fuel to a strategic asset for fuel security."
Platts assessed jet fuel cargoes on a CIF basis in Northwest Europe at $1,603.5/metric ton Oct. 1, up 93% from Feb. 27, before the war in the Middle East upended jet fuel trade flows. Platts assessed SAF on an equivalent basis at $2,903.25/mt, up 27% over the same timeframe. The spread between jet and CIF has narrowed from 176% before the war to 81% Oct. 1, Platts data showed.

Naphtha imports fall Singapore's imports of naphtha, reformates and other
gasoline blendstocks fell 33.35% week over week to 178,462 mt in the week ended Sept. 30, the data showed.
Malaysia was the largest supplier, accounting for 35,737 mt of arrivals, followed closely by Algeria, at 35,109 mt.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

9

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 2, 2026

While the end goal remains the broad adoption of clean fuels, the geopolitical landscape has accelerated the transition by framing SAF as a security tool rather than just a sustainability one, Schutte said.
Global SAF demand is forecast to reach 80,000 barrels/day in 2027, up from 62,000 b/d in 2026, driven by higher demand in the UK and Asia, analysts at S&P Global Energy Horizon said Sept. 7.
Policy remains the main driver of SAF adoption, with uneven ambitions at the regional level, Ina Chirita, associate director, biofuels analytics at S&P Global Energy, said on a webinar Sept. 30. "Although the Strait of Hormuz has created the incentives for more biofuels blending in general, we have not really seen this facilitating a higher adoption of SAF," Chirita said.
Logistics bottleneck Despite strategic momentum, critical regulatory friction within
European pipeline infrastructure risks undermining the scale-up, Schutte said.
One issue is the Central Europe Pipeline System, or CEPS, the NATO-operated multi-connected pipeline network linking major fuel import hubs, including Rotterdam, to key aviation centres such as Amsterdam's Schiphol Airport. Differing interpretations across EU member states of how SAF deliveries into CEPS should be treated are creating a critical choke point.
"Current European rules on traceability in multi-connected pipeline systems, such as NATO's Central Europe Pipeline System, restrict efficient transport from primary import hubs like Rotterdam to major aviation hubs like Schiphol," Schutte said. "Different member states interpret delivery into CEPS differently."
Vopak advocates a book-and-claim approach within logistics networks as the most operationally and commercially effective solution. Under such a system, the sustainable attributes of a fuel -- such as its carbon-reduction credentials -- would be decoupled from the physical movement of molecules, allowing supply and demand to be matched administratively rather than requiring SAF to physically travel to each specific point of use.
This approach would deliver three concrete benefits, she said. These are eliminating inefficient physical routing to hubs where SAF is available; enabling economies of scale by allowing blending in larger volumes at major hubs, thereby lowering overall transport costs; and streamlining accounting processes while preventing double-counting of sustainability credits.
"To keep clean fuels affordable and flowing, regulatory rules need full harmonization within the EU and therewith acceptance of at least mass balancing across pipeline networks," Schutte said, adding that for CEPS entry specifically, this means "decoupling administrative traceability from physical molecule tracking."
The ReFuelEU Aviation regulation requires SAF to account for 2% of aviation fuel supplied at EU airports from 2025, rising to 70% by 2050.

Mandate momentum Despite the regulatory complexity, Vopak's own experience in
the Netherlands points to strong underlying demand when the right incentives are in place. In 2025, driven by national incentive schemes known as HBEs -- Hernieuwbare Brandstofeenheden, or Renewable Fuel Units -- the Netherlands recorded voluntary SAF blending volumes that significantly exceeded the volumes required under the ReFuelEU mandate, Schutte said.
The broader market will ultimately be mandate-driven, with voluntary uptake remaining a localized and secondary force, Schutte said. "Long-term regulatory stability is a non-negotiable prerequisite for developing physical supply chain infrastructure, and even more important to ensure producers and endconsumers will invest in the new SAF production technology. Shifting target lines creates market uncertainty that halts investment decisions right when infrastructure needs to scale," she said.
The European Commission had made its decarbonization trajectory clear, and proposals such as the 2027 revision of the EU Emissions Trading System reaffirm that aviation decarbonization remains a top policy priority, she said.
"While we recognize the gap between ambition and current production levels, the upcoming review of ReFuelEU Aviation should also focus on resolving logistical bottlenecks and setting realistic operational rules," Schutte said.
-- Thomas Washington

The volume of Singapore jet fuel/kerosene swaps traded during the Platts Market on Close assessment process inched up 2.9% month over month to 1.8 million barrels in September 2026, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed Oct. 1.
However, the traded volume posted a 21.7% decline year over year compared with 2.3 million barrels transacted in September 2025.
Meanwhile, the traded volume of Singapore MOC regrade swaps rebounded from 200,000 barrels in August to 900,000 barrels in September, gaining 5.88% year over year, according to Platts data.

Platts assessed the front-month derivative time spread for FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene swaps -- an indicator of the near-term market outlook -- at an average of plus $3.50/b in September, widening from plus $2.89/b in August, Platts data showed.

-- Sophia Aung
Russia could partially open diesel exports in case of surplus output: Novak
Deputy PM says exports to begin after return to surplus
Putin previously said sanctions would stop diesel exports
Diesel export ban to stay in place until end of October

The European diesel market continues to weigh the possibility of US diesel export restrictions, despite experts advising against it.
"The question is whether it is going to happen fully, or a partial ban or export restrictions," said an European diesel trader.
"Europe has been counting a lot on US barrels and if anything happens -- either an export quota, ban or hurricane -- it will have a huge impact and Europe will see another [price] spike," another European diesel trader said.
"A full ban is not needed... a full ban will cause more disruption than an export quota... which would achieve some sort of local relief with plunging the market," the second trader said.
The second trader also said that with talks of a US ban, "China wants to make sure that they can cover their own demand, like at the beginning of the war, so they are reducing exports."
This will likely constrict the availability of product in the Asian market and product that can be arbitraged to Europe, further buoying European diesel prices.
"Asian cracks should do well in the current environment as Europe prices to attract what barrels it can," especially in the case of absent US flows, another European diesel market participant said.
Last week, European diesel prices spiked as US President Donald Trump appeared to be endorsing the prospect of a US diesel export ban, with the goal of bringing some relief to American consumers.
The front-month ICE low sulfur gasoil futures rose $60/metric ton to $1,490/mt on Sept. 23, before rising to near all- time highs at $1,519.24/mt on Sept. 24, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed.
A third European diesel trader said that the heating oil market in the US had already "factored in a ban... on Thursday and Friday [Sept. 24-25]."

Russia could consider partially opening diesel exports once it returns to a production surplus, Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak told its Federation Council Oct. 2, describing the market as balanced and sufficiently supplied, local media reported.
Traditionally the world's number-two diesel exporter, Russia started shutting in its diesel production in July following persistent Ukrainian drone attacks on its refining sector, now extended until the end of October. For non-producers, which include traders and refineries with capacities of less than 1 million metric tons/year, the ban will remain in place until the end of January.
The deputy prime minister added that given Russia produces more diesel than it consumes, it is necessary to avoid accumulating more stocks than the country has the capacity to store, which would force refineries to reduce throughput and affect the output of other products.
Novak's comments come a day after Russia's President Vladimir Putin said at the Valday Forum in Moscow that Russia had sufficient diesel but warned the fuel wouldn't make it to international markets because of foreign sanctions.
"We already have enough diesel fuel. But it won't reach global markets because of the bans and sanctions against our oil and petroleum products," Putin was reported to have said at the forum, according to the transcript posted on the Kremlin website.
By the end of August, analysts at S&P Global Energy CERA estimated that almost half of Russia's primary refining capacity was offline due to Ukrainian drone strikes, with persistent and targeted attacks often hitting the same sites repeatedly during the month. On Oct. 1, Putin estimated that Ukrainian strikes had shaved off about 1% of Russia's GDP.
Novak also said Oct. 2 that once the fuel market is balanced, Russia will return to consuming its own gasoline and exporting

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

October 2, 2026

it to other countries. Russia produces around 10%-15% more gasoline than it consumes, he said, calling the imports a temporary measure.
Gasoline was the first type of fuel export banned by the Kremlin back in April, and the restriction has since been extended through to the end of January 2027. The measure has seen Russia turn to foreign suppliers to import local fuel supply, exacerbating global concerns over international tightness.
He added that Russia will not stop attacks on Ukrainian ships and sensitive sites in exchange for halting Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries.
The Russian president's comments come amid growing international concerns over rising diesel prices, particularly from the administration of US President Donald Trump, ahead of the country's upcoming midterm elections.
Speaking to local broadcasters Sept. 27, Trump recently called the price spikes "a Russia problem" rather than a consequence of Middle Eastern trade shut-ins, and urged Ukraine to pull back drone attacks targeting the country's refineries.
-- Elza Turner, Kelly Norways
ARA diesel, gasoil stocks rise 11% on week: Insights Global
Stocks build amid Rhine disruptions
Product accumulates at ARA with restricted vessel flows
Diesel and gasoil stocks in the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp refining hub rose 180,000 metric tons, or 11%, to 1.816 million mt in the week ended Oct.1, the latest data from Insights Global showed.
However, inventories remain depleted 393,000 mt, or 18%, from the same week in 2025.
Stock building in the ARA hub comes against a backdrop of constrained supply in Europe and market anxiety surrounding a potential US diesel export ban. Therefore, rising stock levels are unlikely to be a sign of easing fundamentals in Northwest Europe.
"The extremely low water levels on the Rhine could be a major explanation for the build-up of gasoil and fuel oil inventories in ARA. With barges unable to carry normal loads, transportation further inland, particularly to Germany, is constrained. As a result, products may accumulate in ARA while inventories further inland in Europe are being depleted," said Arne Lohmann Rasmussen, chief analyst at Global Risk Management, in a research note on Oct. 2.
"We haven't been able to load anything in ARA or Germany because of low water levels, so we've not been able to do anything in the market, even though demand is picking up for winter and there is product to load," said a European distillates trader.
In the morning of Oct. 2, Rhine water levels hovered around 0 centimeters at the chokepoint of Kaub, according to data from Germany's Directorate-General for Waterways and Shipping.
Disruptions to shipping logistics have further bolstered diesel

and gasoil barge prices, on top of curtailed flows from Russia and the Middle East amid ongoing geopolitical conflicts in the respective regions.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 10 parts per million FOB ARA barge flat price at $1,445/mt on Oct. 1, while 50 ppm barges stood at $1,425.50/mt.
The market's attention is now focused on the impact of potential US export restrictions.
"The question is whether it is going to happen fully, or as a partial ban or quotas," said a European diesel trader.
"Europe has been counting a lot on US barrels. If anything happens- either an export quota, ban or hurricane- it will have a huge impact and Europe will see another spike," said a second European diesel trader.

Australia was the largest destination for Singapore's gasoil exports in the week ended Sept. 30, receiving 171,168 mt, followed by the Netherlands with 39,960 mt and Vietnam with 33,373 mt, according to the data.

Platts Singapore MOC gasoil swaps traded volume rises 64% MOM in Sep
Total gasoil swaps trades up 15.7% YOY
Russian diesel ban extended to end-Oct
The volume of Singapore gasoil swaps traded during Platts Market on Close assessment process increased by 63.89% month over month and 15.69% year over year to 5.9 million barrels in September, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed Oct. 1. The traded volume rebounded from an eightmonth low of 3.6 million barrels in August.
The Platts-assessed FOB Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil frontmonth derivative time spread -- an indicator of the near-term market outlook -- averaged $7.01/b in September, widening from $5.79/b in August, Platts data showed.
The Asian gasoil complex saw some tightening as Russia extended its complete ban on diesel exports for another month, through Oct. 31, further tightening global supplies.
The ban was initially valid until the end of August but was extended to the end of September against the background of shortages due to ongoing Ukrainian strikes on refineries. The ban on non-producers is enforced until the end of January 2027.
Meanwhile, Platts assessed the front-month gasoil exchange of futures for swaps -- the spread between Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil swaps and the corresponding ICE low sulfur gasoil futures contract -- at an average of minus $148.39/metric ton in September, compared with minus $106.27/mt in August, Platts data showed.
A more negative EFS spread generally points toward a more lucrative East-West arbitrage flow.
Across the month, concerns over a potential US diesel export ban, which could further tighten global supplies and exacerbate the European diesel shortage, pushed the Platts-assessed frontmonth gasoil EFS to an all-time low at the Asian close Sept. 23, the lowest level since Platts began assessing the contract on May 18, 2009.
Platts assessed the gasoil EFS -- the spread between Singapore 10 parts per million sulfur gasoil swaps and the corresponding ICE low-sulfur gasoil futures contract -- at minus $199.82/mt Sept. 23, widening $37.13/mt day over day.
The EFS rebounded by the end of September to minus

$140.60/mt at the Asian close Oct. 1, following signals that a full US diesel ban was unlikely.
Toward the end of September, cargo flows in the Middle East were heard to be easing. Combined with higher North Asian supplies and softer demand expectations in the region, the Asian gasoil complex saw some profit-taking toward the end of the month, according to a Southeast Asia-based trade source.
The Platts-assessed front-month Singapore gasoil swap crack against the front-month Dubai swap averaged $74.38/b in September, up from $67.42/b in August, and up from $61.79/b in July, Platts data showed.
-- Rachelle Teo, Mei huey Ng, Wei xiang Ang

BUNKERWORLD INDEX: HSFO, VLSFO see mixed price trends
Limited HSFO imports to Singapore
Lower refinery output hobbles Hamburg HSFO supply
Volatile USGC VLSFO amid limited exports
Marine fuel prices saw divergent trends through the week ending Oct. 1 as very low sulfur declined slightly, tracking earlier crude movements, while high sulfur fuel oil climbed on continued tight supply.
The Platts Bunkerworld Marine Fuel 0.5% Sulfur Index ended the week on Oct. 1 at $862/metric ton, up $18/mt day over day, down $5/mt week over week and up $75/mt month over month. The Platts BW380 Index, which represents value for 3.5% sulfur fuel oil, ended the week at $728/mt, up $21/mt day over day, up $20/mt week over week and up $108/mt month over month. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Crude oil futures slipped throughout the week but then recovered at the end to finish slightly above where they started, as concerns about potential escalation in the Middle East overshadowed rising Persian Gulf oil flows.
Crude prices surged to the day's high late in the session on Oct. 1 after the Wall Street Journal reported that US President Donald Trump has told aides the US will resume attacks on Iran following the Nov. 3 midterm elections. The Wall Street Journal also reported that the US was deploying a third aircraft carrier and up to 10,000 additional troops to the Middle East.
Bunker prices have proved a burden for shipping. "In the third quarter, Stolt Tankers' earnings declined year over year, but were stable versus the prior quarter, with firmer average freight rates offset by lower volumes and higher bunker costs," Udo Lange, Chief Executive Officer of Stolt-Nielsen Limited, said in a statement Oct. 1.

ARA fuel oil stocks climb 18.5% on week to 961,000 mt: Insights Global
HSFO crack spread narrows to minus $11.60/b
Blending components eases supply constraints
Fuel oil stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub increased by 18.5% to 961,000 metric tons in the week to Oct. 1, according to Insights Global data, after two consecutive weeks of draws.
Fuel oil's share of overall oil product inventories in the ARA region climbed to 22%.
In the Northwest European high sulfur fuel oil market, participants have expressed a landscape defined by muted demand.

"Demand is not wild at the moment, supply is ok," a trader said.
On the supply side, the market remained free of availability issues, traders said, with steady flows from the west arriving throughout September.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month HSFO crack spread at minus $11.60/barrel, down $2.069/b from minus $9.531/b the previous week.
Within the Northwest European very low sulfur fuel oil market, market participants have described a greater availability of blending components that can be sold into the cracked fuel oil pool, despite some tightness for lower sulfur material.
"The blending component issue is a bit easier, but people still lack 0.1%-0.3%S material," said a trader.
Other sources indicated that the increased availability of feedstock products had made blending into the cracked fuel oil pool more economical.
While market participants indicated that the VLSFO arbitrage window out of Europe was open toward the end of September, persistently higher freight costs have put pressure on this arbitrage, which could keep more volumes locally in Europe.
In the paper market, the front-month EU fuel oil 0.5%S marine fuel oil structure was assessed at a backwardation of $12.75/mt on Oct. 2, unchanged week over week.
The 3.5%S fuel oil FOB Rotterdam barge front-month time spread equivalent was assessed at a backwardation of $15.25/mt on Oct. 2, narrowing $6.50/mt week over week.
Within the ARA hub, fuel oil availability tightened as limited blending components constrained supply and disrupted loading operations in Rotterdam and Amsterdam.
Despite the broader tightness, some market participants continued to offer prompt HSFO and VLSFO deliveries, highlighting differing supply positions across the market.
Demand signals were mixed as the EU's Renewable Energy Directive III compliance costs continued to weigh on demand in Rotterdam, with bunker buyers increasingly opting for alternative ports that do not face the same compliance burden.
However, market participants reported fluctuating demand as buyers reacted quickly to price movements.
"People are doing bunkers four to six days ahead," a trader said, adding, "due to the volatile geopolitical situation and futures market, people want to fix at the last moment."

Singapore MOC fuel oil swaps trades rise 66% MOM in Sep to four-year high
HSFO swaps trades rise 98.7% MOM in Sep
LSFO swaps trades fall about 20% from Aug
Singapore fuel oil derivative volumes traded during the Platts Market on Close assessment process in September rose 65.8% month over month to 27.12 million barrels, as high sulfur fuel oil deals almost doubled, according to Platts data.
The total volume of fuel oil swaps trades, which fell to a two-month low in August, rebounded to their highest monthly level since September 2022, when 34.19 million barrels of fuel oil swaps were traded, the data showed. Trades in September more than tripled year over year, with 7.37 million barrels traded in September 2025, according to the data.
Singapore HSFO swaps trades rose 98.7% month over month to 23.46 million barrels in September, marking the highest monthly level for the residual grade since September 2022, when 31.62 million barrels of HSFO were traded, the data showed.
Of the total HSFO swaps traded in September, 20.16 million barrels were benchmark 380 CST HSFO trades, up from 7.37 million barrels in August, according to the data. In contrast, the volume of 180 CST HSFO trades declined by about 29% month over month to 1.33 million barrels.
Traded volumes rose amid marginally weaker time spreads for Singapore HSFO swaps. The Mo1-Mo2 intermonth spread for the 380 CST HSFO grade averaged a backwardation of $31.27/metric ton in September, compared with $31.82/mt in the preceding month, Platts data showed.
The Asian HSFO market has been supported by tighter supply and concerns about disruptions from the conflict in the Middle East, according to trade sources. Coupled with relatively stable bunker demand, HSFO fundamentals may attract pockets of utility-sector demand amid worries about LNG availability, trade sources said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo cash differential against the MOPS 380 CST HSFO assessment at a premium of $44.75/mt at the Asian close Oct. 1, up from $40.67/mt on Sept. 30, buoyed by firmer bids from Shell for second-half October-loading cargoes.
Meanwhile, Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S swaps trade volume fell about 20% month over month to 3.65 million barrels in September, Platts data showed.
The Mo1-Mo2 intermonth spread for FOB Singapore 0.5%S marine fuel swaps averaged a backwardation of $40.64/mt in September, compared with $36.45/mt in August, Platts data showed.

"HSFO availabilities in Piraeus are tight until Sept. 28, and barge schedules are busy," a supplier said Sept. 24.

ARA naphtha stocks fall to 34-month low, down 12.91% week over week
Rhine logistical bottlenecks curb deliveries
Gasoline flows to Med on tight oxy supply
Naphtha inventories in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub fell to a multiyear low in the week to Oct. 1, despite market participants pointing to softer European blending demand.
ARA naphtha stocks dropped 39,000 metric tons, or 12.91%, week over week to 263,000 mt, down from 302,000 mt on Sept. 24, according to Insights Global data. The draw took stocks to their lowest level since January 2024, a 34-month low.
The sharp stock draw was reflected in a firmer prompt market structure. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the frontmonth CIF NWE naphtha crack at minus $5.30/barrel on Oct. 1, up

41 cents/b day over day, with the equivalent swap down $4.25/mt at $823.50/mt.
The October-November time spread was assessed at $29.50/ mt on the same day, widening $5.50/mt, while the NovemberDecember spread also rose $5.50/mt to $30/mt, pointing to a steepening backwardation amid tighter prompt availability.
The fall comes as regional supply remains constrained, with market participants continuing to cite logistical bottlenecks along the Rhine, even as demand from gasoline blenders has cooled.
Water levels at Kaub were printed at 0 cm on Oct. 2 CET and are forecast to remain in the mid-single digits next week, according to data from the German water authority WSV.
Persistently low river levels are restricting naphtha deliveries and complicating the transport of finished products.
"For naphtha, [I'm] hearing [there are] still issues with logistical bottlenecks, the Kaub water level [is] printing negative," a trading source said.
However, the demand pull for naphtha has been slowing. Gasoline blending demand has eased as the market moves away from the peak summer driving season, while high flat prices have also reduced buying appetite.
"Naphtha is relatively quieter, [I'm] hearing gasoline indeed cooled off a bit, also in line with seasonality," the same trading source said.
The weaker demand for naphtha from the blending pool was also linked to shortages of high-octane blendstocks like MTBE.
"I think that demand in general is softer because of the prolonged high flat price, but I think also there are not enough high octanes around. That means that naphtha has naturally lower demand for blenders," a second trading source said.
"[The] last I'd heard [it] was due to lack of octane," a third trading source said.
Arbitrage economics from Northwest Europe are also difficult, particularly into the US Atlantic Coast and West Africa, due to high freight costs, further reducing blending requirements in Northwest Europe.
"I think that WAF is not buying that much," the second trading source said.
"[I'm] hearing freight in Europe is keeping the arb into USAC and WAF tricky for gasoline," the first trading source said.
Despite this, gasoline stocks in the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp hub declined 12.4% week over week to 890,000 mt on Oct. 1, as supplies were sent from the NWE market into the Mediterranean.
A total of 184,400 mt of gasoline will be discharged in the Med region next week, starting Oct. 5, after having been loaded in the ARA hub, according to ship tracking data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Most of these volumes loaded in the week of Sept. 28, which is the main cause of the sharp drawdown.
This is the largest single-week volume that flowed along this route this year, according to CAS.
"There is a huge lack of oxy molecules," a gasoline trader said. "Therefore, Med is very tight."

Gas Liquids
India's September LPG imports from Middle East highest since start of war: CAS
Middle East LPG shipments to India rise 20% MOM
US LPG exports to India decrease 38% in Sept.
UAE overtakes US as India's top LPG supplier
India took delivery of the highest volume of LPG from the Middle East in September since the start of the Middle East war and the disruption to cargo flows via the Strait of Hormuz in late February, according to the latest S&P Commodities at Sea data on Oct. 2, while the volume of LPG inflows from the US fell by more than 38% from a month ago to its lowest since the start of the war.
India imported 955,400 mt of LPG, comprising 449,000 mt propane and 506,000 mt butane, from the Middle East in September, up 20.34% from a month earlier. The Middle East contributed 64.99% to India's overall imports in September, up from 39.51% in August, with the largest volume of imports arriving from the UAE at 485,400 mt, followed by Oman at 238,000 mt, Iran at 135,700 mt and Qatar at 96,300 mt, the CAS data showed.
Since the start of tensions in the Middle East, the US has been India's largest LPG supplier until September, when the top spot was overtaken by the UAE. India imported 478,800 mt of LPG from the US last month, down 38.34% from August when the latter exported 776,500 mt of LPG to India, the data showed.
According to a report released Sept. 29 by analysts at CAS, US LPG exports declined approximately 27% week over week to about 1.3 million mt in the week ended Sept. 25, reflecting weaker loading activity across key US Gulf Coast terminals.
"The reduction appeared to be driven by logistical constraints, including Panama Canal restrictions and vessel rerouting via the Cape of Good Hope, rather than weaker demand," the report showed.
It added that following higher flows in the previous week, US LPG exports for the week ended Sept. 25 eased slightly amid ship availability and scheduling constraints.
"The decline was led by shipments to the US-Far East route, which fell nearly 29% week over week to 612,000 mt, while no LPG volumes were directed to India," the report showed.
India took delivery of 1.470 million mt of LPG, comprising 760,000 mt of propane and 710,000 mt of butane, in September, falling 7.66% from a month earlier and 31.69% from a year ago.
-- Norazlina Jumaat

Record-high freight rate for Houston-Japan VLGCs sees weakening from extra Panama Canal transits
Panama Canal adds two daily VLGC vessel slots
USGC-Japan VLGC freight costs peak at $330/mt
Drought delays persist despite transit increase
The freight rate to ship VLGC propane and butane carriers from the US Gulf Coast to Japan entered October at a record high, but showed signs of weakening on news of more daily transits allowed through the Panama Canal.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the key freight rate from Houston to Chiba, Japan, at $330/metric ton for the fifth straight trading day Oct. 1, breaking the previous high of $326/mt on Aug. 8-12, 2014, almost one year after Platts created the assessment. The other major route for VLGCs out of the Gulf Coast -- to Northwest Europe -- remained at $178/mt, just $0.25/ mt below its all-time high.
In late trading Oct. 1, however, the USGC-Japan freight rate was heard to have dropped to $315/mt. S&P Global Energy CERA analyst Edgar Ang said this week's decision to restore the daily Panama Canal limit to 10 LPG vessels from the current eight on Oct. 15 was seen as a positive.
"That's good news, but that's going to take a while for things to get back to normal," Ang said Oct. 1. "But the upper limit for freight is probably pretty close, with the canal adding extra slots."
CERA projected an average freight rate in 2027 at $200/mt, noting that an extra 47 VLGC newbuilds will add to the global fleet of 485 VLGC and VLEC ships and that Mideast supply would return once the Iran war ends. But Panama continues to be troubled by drought, which slows canal passage and incentivizes journeys around Argentina, which are over three weeks longer.
"$200 is still pretty high," Ang said. "The history is less than $100. The freight rate will come off, but not drastically. The canal issue is not going to go away."
The propane FOB premium over Mt. Belvieu was unchanged at 9 cents/gal, while Enterprise propane rose 0.375 cent/gal to 86.75 cents/gal, butane rose 1 cent to 134.75 cents/gal, ethane rose 0.25 cent to 26.375 cents/gal and natural gasoline spiked 12 cents to 209.25 cents/gal at Enterprise Mt. Belvieu. CERA noted that butane export conditions remain challenging, with firm butane prices and the high freight constraining the arbitrage.

LR2 tightness drives charterers to MR fixtures on Med-Japan clean route
MR fixtures to Japan more than double in 2026
LR2 freight hits $8.375 mil Mediterranean-Japan
Four LR2 vessels switch to dirty trade this week

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 2, 2026

nine months of 2026, as rising long-range vessel freight pushed charterers toward smaller alternatives on one of the clean tanker market's most closely watched long-haul routes.
The shift reflected a tightening clean LR2 tonnage list as owners moved vessels to alternative employment, with a Londonbased broker identifying four LR2s that switched out of clean trading during the week. The development has implications for clean product trade flows between Europe and Asia, where LR2s have traditionally been the vessel of choice for large-volume cargoes.
Fleet rebalancing MR tanker fixtures on the Mediterranean-to-Japan route
reached 25 in the first nine months of 2026, more than double the 12 recorded for the whole of 2025, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea. The increase indicated a measurable change in vessel selection on the route, with charterers turning to MRs as a readily available, cost-competitive alternative as LR2 freight rose.
A Europe-based source said MRs were much cheaper than LR2s because of tight LR2 availability in the region.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed LR2 Mediterranean-to-Japan freight via the Suez Canal at $8.375 million lump sum on Oct. 1, the highest since May 2026.
A London-based broker identified four LR2 vessels that switched out of clean trading in a single week, citing the pull of the dirty petroleum product market.
"Current DPP USG-Med and USG-UKC fixing levels are high, so owners [are] preferring the DPP market. This has led to the LR2 list tightening up, and Med-Far East freight firming in line with the Aframax market," the broker said. "List is pretty tight for LR2s in the Med," the same source said.
Market participants characterized the use of MRs on longerhaul movements as an availability- and cost-driven response rather than a structural replacement for LR employment, with charterers fixing the vessel class that is available depending on tonnage position, cargo requirements, routing, and freight economics.
Due to the sharp rise in naphtha prices, and overall freight, and lesser demand by petrochemical units, end-receivers, and importers prefer to take in smaller parcels. This has also contributed to greater use of MRs to move cargoes, North Asiabased charterers said.
Some relief may be on the horizon. Around 20 LR2s are expected to be available in Oman and West Coast India for loading over the next three weeks, sources said.
The London-based broker said "resupply of vessels to come in the next few weeks," adding that this may ease the tightness.
However, not all vessels are expected to return to Europe -- some will reload and head back East, while others may open in the Mediterranean for their next cargo, depending on freight levels and daily earnings at that time, other market participants said.
The Suez Canal remains the preferred passage for Mediterranean-to-Japan voyages, with a London-based source

saying it is "hard to find a vessel that wants to go through the Cape of Good Hope."
Cape of Good Hope routing commands a premium, though no fixed differential versus Suez can be identified, as the decision is highly owner- and vessel-dependent, sources said. Additional voyage costs, including the Additional War Risk Premium, are generally reflected directly in lump-sum freight and are on the charterers' account, sources said.
The LR1 market adds a further layer of complexity to the clean tanker picture in Europe. The pool of LR1 vessels available in the region has diminished as owners increasingly deploy tonnage on Brazil and US Gulf runs, the London-based source said. The LR1 list was also described as tight, compounding the pressure on charterers seeking alternatives to LR tonnage and leaving MRs as the most readily available option for some longer-haul movements.
-- Alara Tizer, Sameer Mohindru
Americas Suezmax, Aframax rates hit record highs on bullish fundamentals
Market strengthens on tight tonnage, more inquiry
Suezmax Guyana-UKC freight rises 129% in one week
Aframax USGC-UKC route hits new peak of w950
The Americas dirty tanker segment saw Suezmax and Aframax rates reach all-time highs on Oct. 2 as a tight tonnage list in the Atlantic Basin met increased inquiry levels, leading to shipowners securing significantly higher rates.
Tonnage in the Atlantic Basin remained diminished, as a steady stream of cargoes originally intended for Very Large Crude Carriers were split into multiple Suezmax cargoes; multiple brokers reported an overall lack of VLCCs in the Atlantic, with most largely still positioned in the East. This left charterers competing for a limited number of Suezmax options, putting owners in the driver's seat during negotiations.

"I think at this point, owners [are] only limited by their imagination," a shipbroker said on Oct. 1.

Three hijacked tankers freed from Somali pirates, but five seafarers killed: IMO
Five crew members die during rescue operation
First crew casualties from Somali piracy in over a decade
Houthis, Somali pirates threaten maritime trade
Three tankers hijacked by pirates in the Gulf of Aden were freed this week after months in captivity, but five seafarers were killed during one of the rescue operations, the International Maritime Organization said Oct. 2.
The five crew members died during a Sept. 29 operation to free the 3,089-dwt Honour 25 in Somali waters, while several others were seriously injured, according to a statement from the UN agency. These appear to be the first crew casualties from Somali piracy in over a decade.
Aside from the product tanker, Somai authorities also rescued the 32,200-dwt Sibu 1 and the 3,313-dwt Eureka and their crews. The ships were captured by pirates between April and August.

"Difficult to convey in words how distressing it is to continuously hear that seafarers are losing their lives simply for doing their job," IMO Secretary-General Arsenio Dominguez said in a statement. "I am deeply saddened to learn of the tragic loss of five seafarers."
The IMO warned that piracy remains an active threat in the region, as 22 seafarers remain captive aboard the 10,960-dwt tanker Asana that was hijacked earlier this year.
Dominguez urged governments to step up maritime security cooperation and support initiatives aimed at protecting commercial shipping, highlighting the role of the EU's Aspides and Atalanta naval operations in the Red Sea, Gulf of Aden and western Indian Ocean.
The IMO also called on shipowners and operators transiting the region to undertake security risk assessments and implement industry best management practices to reduce piracy risks.
"We have come together to stop piracy in this region before. We must do it again --- with renewed resolve, targeted cooperation and the required resources," Dominguez said.
While the number of Somali piracy incidents remains far below its peak in 2011, dozens of seafarers have been kidnapped during the recent resurgence.
The International Maritime Bureau, part of the International Chamber of Commerce, recorded six such security incidents off Somalia in January-June, compared with three in the same period of 2025 and seven in 2024.
But Somali pirates took hostage 63 crew members during the period, accounting for 94% of all seafarers captured globally, according to an earlier IMB report.
The Gulf of Aden remains a key transit route for crude oil, refined products and containerized trade moving between Asia, Europe and the Mediterranean via the Suez Canal. The resurgence in Somali piracy has added a further layer of security risk for shipowners already navigating heightened threats from Houthi attacks in the Red Sea.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the MR tanker rate for transporting 35,000 metric tons of clean petroleum products from the Red Sea to Japan at $218.03 per metric ton on Oct. 2, up 242% from $63.71/mt on Dec. 31.
-- Max Lin

Similar to the mandate in mid-March, clean oil products will only be exported to Hong Kong, Macau and jet fuel barrels to China's international airports, a northern China-based refining source said.
Although cargoes that have already obtained customs declaration approval remain eligible for loading, clean oil product exports are still expected to slump in October, the sources said.
Sinopec and PetroChina did not immediately reply to a request for official comment.
Market analysts and traders said this move by Chinese refineries has boosted regional oil product cracks and is likely to keep them elevated unless market fundamentals change.
The Platts FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene cargo crack spread against front-month cash Dubai, which measures the relative strength of the product to the crude it is refined from, surged $10.93/b day over day to $74.51/b on Oct. 1. It surpassed the physical crack spread for FOB Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil, which rose $5.22/b day over day to $72.14/b on Oct. 1.
Retightening controls A 35,000-mt ultra low sulfur diesel cargo scheduled to load
over Oct. 13-15 from northeastern China to Australia was withdrawn from shipping fixtures, along with a 35,000-mt jet fuel cargo that was fixed to load from southern China over Oct. 14-16 to the same destination, according to a Singapore-based shipping source.
"Our cargo to Vietnam, which is scheduled to load in the coming days, can still be loaded because it has already obtained customs declaration approval. However, cargoes without such approval will need to wait for further instructions," a South Chinabased refining source said.
Two traders said they would have to source alternative supplies, without disclosing the products involved, as China suspended exports to foreign countries and the timeline for a resumption remains unclear.
The traders estimated about 2 million mt of clean oil product exports in October, including shipments to foreign destinations, Hong Kong and Macau, as well as jet fuel supplied for bonded bunkering at the country's international airports, falling from the previous expectation of 3.85 million mt.
In comparison, market sources estimated the exports at 4.36 million mt in September, and Chinese customs reported 4.58 million mt of exports in August.

Resumption unclear "Exports could resume at any time with just a phone call from
the authorities. But both state-owned and independent refineries are set to further cut throughput in November because high freight rates have discouraged crude purchases for November delivery," the South China-based refining source said.
"Oil product supplies will be tighter then, forcing more barrels to stay in the domestic market," he added.
Chinese refineries are required to prioritize meeting domestic supply, Platts reported earlier.
Several refining sources told Platts in late September that refineries would remain profitable only if freight costs for crude cargoes stayed below $5/b under China's gasoline and gasoil pricing mechanism.
However, freight for Middle Eastern crude cargoes has recently hovered around $20/b, while freight for West African cargoes was around $15-$16/b, discouraging refining interest, the sources said. They added that even state-owned refiners were seeking to lower utilization rates or shut units for maintenance.
Crude utilization at China's state-owned refineries is likely to lose momentum in October, falling from 76.4% in September and 76% in August, amid limited crude availability, Platts reported earlier.
Dirty freight for a 270,000-mt VLCC carrying crude from the Gulf region to China has hovered around $230/mt since Sept. 16, according to Platts. The assessment rose 1.36% day over day to $232.85/mt on Oct. 1, compared to $135.14/mt on Sept. 1. The assessment hit a record high of $239.09/mt on Sept. 8, according to Platts data.
Separately, Platts reported that the third-batch quota for oil product exports was released Sept. 30, though further details were not immediately available.

G7 orders 100 million barrel oil stock release, rejects energy export bans
Deadline set for last tranche of IEA commitment
Members to frontload 'substantial' diesel supplies
Members commit to avoid energy export restrictions
The G7 has set a four-month deadline for its member states to release the last 100 million barrels of oil stocks they committed to in March after collectively pledging to refrain from energy export restrictions within the organization.
In a statement Oct. 2, the seven industrial countries called on the International Energy Agency to "monitor the immediate and full implementation of the March 2026 commitments." Adjusting for stocks already released to the market, the group pledged to oversee the release of 100 million barrels of oil by February next year.
The decision included a commitment for the G7 and partner countries to frontload a "substantial diesel release" in the next 20 days, with members to discuss the possibility of additional releases in coordination with the IEA. The group will also seek to coordinate
